**Exhibit D**

This description of the Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela is dated as of December 21, 2017, and appears as
Exhibit D to the Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela’s Annual Report on Form 18-K to the United States Securities and Exchange Commission for the fiscal year ended December 31, 2016.

**DEFINED TERMS**

Unless otherwise specified or the context requires otherwise, all references to the following defined terms have the meanings specified:

• “ANC” refers to the National Constituent Assembly (Asamblea Nacional Constituyente);

• “Banco Central” refers to the Venezuelan Central Bank (Banco Central de Venezuela);

• “Central Government” refers to the national and federal branches of the government of the Republic;

• “CLAPs” refers to the Local Committees of Supply and Production (Comités Locales de Abastecimiento y Producción);

• “Constitution” refers to Constitution of the Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela (Constitución de la República Bolivariana de Venezuela) as adopted on December 15, 1999 and amended on February 15, 2009;

• “decentralized state entities” refers to corporations that are majority or wholly owned by the Republic;

• “Government” refers to the consolidated public sector divided, in general terms, into three parts: the Central Government, the regional and local governments of the Republic and the decentralized state entities;

• “INE” refers to the National Institute of Statistics (Instituto Nacional de Estadística);

• “Ministry of Communications and Information” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Communications and Information (Ministerios del Poder Popular para la Comunicación e Información);

• “Ministry of Culture” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Culture (Ministerio del Poder Popular para la Cultura);

• “Ministry of Defense” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Defense (Ministerios del Poder Popular para la Defensa);

• “Ministry of Economy and Finance” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power of Economy and Finance (Ministerio del Poder Popular de la Economía y Finanzas), resulting from the merger in January 2017 of the Ministry of Popular Power for Banking and Finance (Ministerios del Poder Popular para la Banca y Finanzas) and the Ministry of Popular Power for Industry and Commerce (Ministerios del Poder Popular para la Industria y Comercio) – for convenience, references herein to the ‘Ministry of Economy and Finance’ also refer to the previous Ministries before their combination;

• “Ministry of Electricity” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power of Electricity (Ministerio del Poder Popular para la Energía Eléctrica);

• “Ministry of Essential, Strategic and Socialist Industries” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Essential, Strategic and Socialist Industries (Ministerio del Poder Popular de Industrias Básicas, Estratégicas y Socialistas);

• “Ministry of Fisheries” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power of Fisheries and Aquaculture (Ministerio del Poder Popular de la Pesca y Acuicultura);

• “Ministry of Food” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Food (Ministerio del Poder Popular para la Alimentación);

• “Ministry of Foreign Trade and International Investment” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Foreign Trade and International Investment (Ministerio del Poder Popular para el Comercio Exterior e Inversión Internacional);

• “Ministry of Housing and Habitat” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Housing and Habitat (Ministerio del Poder Popular para Hábitat y Vivienda);

• “Ministry of the Interior” refers to the Ministry of Interior Affairs, Justice and Peace (Ministerio del Poder Popular para Relaciones Interiores, Justicia y Paz);

• “Ministry of Mining” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Ecological Mining Development (Ministerio del Poder Popular de Desarollo Minero Ecológico); in November 2011, the Ministry of Mining succeeded the Ministry of Popular Power for Mining and Essential Industries (Ministerio del Poder Popular para las Industrias Básicas y Minería) – for convenience, references herein to the ‘Ministry of Mining’ also refer to the previous Ministry before its restructure;

• “Ministry of Petroleum” refers to Ministry of Popular Power of Petroleum (Ministerio del Poder Popular de Petróleo), formerly known as Ministry of Popular Power for Oil and Mining (Ministerio del Poder Popular de Petróleo y Minería) – for convenience, references herein to the ‘Ministry of Petroleum’ also refer to the previous Ministry before its restructure;

• “Ministry of Productive Agriculture and Land” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Productive Agriculture and Land (Ministerio del Poder Popular para la Agricultura Productiva y Tierras);

• “Ministry of Productive Economy” refers to the Ministry of State for Productive Economy (Ministerio de Estado para la Economía Productiva);

• “Ministry of Public Works” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power of Public Works (Ministerio del Poder Popular de Obras Públicas);

• “Ministry of Transportation” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Transportation (Ministerio del Poder Popular para Transporte);

• “Ministry of Urban Agriculture” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power of Urban Agriculture (Ministerio de Poder Popular de Agricultura Urbana);

• “Ministry of Youth and Sports” refers to the Ministry of Popular Power for Youth and Sports (Ministerio del Poder Popular para Juventud y el Deporte);

• “National Executive” refers to the national executive branch of the Central Government;

• “PDVSA” refers to Petróleos de Venezuela, S.A, the state oil and gas company;

• “Prosecutor General” refers to the Prosecutor General of the Republic (Fiscalía General de la República);

• “Republic” or “Venezuela” refers to the Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela; and

• “Supreme Tribunal” refers to the Venezuelan Supreme Tribunal of Justice (Tribunal Supremo de Justicia).

**CURRENCY OF PRESENTATION**

Unless otherwise specified or the context requires, references to “dollars,” “U.S. dollars,” “U.S.$” and “$” are to
U.S. dollars; references to “Bolívares” and “Bs.” are to Venezuelan Bolívares (“Bolívares”), the currency of the Republic; references to “Euro,” “EUR” and “€” are
to the lawful currency of the European Union; references to “¥” are to Japanese yen; references to “CNY” or “RMB” are to Chinese yuan renminbi; and references to “bpd” are to barrels per day. As used
herein, the term “billion” means one thousand million, or 1,000,000,000, and the term “trillion” means one thousand billion, or 1,000,000,000,000. Historical amounts translated into Bolívares or U.S. dollars have been
converted at historical rates of exchange, unless otherwise stated. Unless otherwise noted herein, all references to Venezuelan Bolívares refer to nominal Bolívares. Certain amounts that appear herein have been rounded for ease of
presentation. Accordingly, figures shown as totals in certain tables may not represent an arithmetical aggregation of the amounts that precede them. Certain tables, as indicated, contain preliminary figures.

Since 2003, foreign currency exchange in Venezuela has been subject to exchange controls that centralize the purchase and sale of foreign currency with the
Venezuelan Central Bank (*Banco Central de Venezuela* or“Banco Central”). The Ministry of Popular Power of Economy and Finance (*Ministerio del Poder Popular de la Economía y Finanzas* or “Ministry of Economy
and Finance”), together with Banco Central, is in charge of setting the exchange rate with respect to the U.S. dollar and other currencies. As of December 30, 2016, there were three exchange systems, which convert currency at different
rates: the protected exchange rate (*operaciones de devisas con tipo de cambio protegido* or “DIPRO”), the Complementary System for Foreign Currency Exchange Administration rate (*Sistema Complementario de Administración de
Divisas* or “SICAD”) and the complementary floating market rate (*operaciones de divisas con tipo de cambio complementario flotante de mercado* or “DICOM”). As of December 30, 2016, the exchange rates were: DIPRO:
Bs.10.00 = U.S.$1.00, SICAD: Bs.13.50 = U.S.$1.00, and DICOM: Bs.673.76 = U.S.$1.00. The exchange rate for public external debt is based on the rate set by the DIPRO rate (ask). Current exchange rates are published daily on Banco Central’s
website; however, the DICOM rate is published under the name “SIMADI” and “Convenio Cambiario No. 33.” For further information concerning the exchange control regime of the Republic and applicable exchange rates during the
periods of presentation, see “*The Venezuelan Economy—Economic Policy and Legislation—Exchange Control Regime*.”

**INTRODUCTION AND RECENT DEVELOPMENTS**

**Recent Social and Political Developments**

Since 2015,
there has been increasing political and social unrest due to shortages of basic consumer goods as a result of a drop in domestic food production; limited access to imports as a result of currency restrictions; smuggling; hoarding and other
distribution problems. In addition, during the same period, Venezuela experienced a severe drought that contributed to an electricity crisis due to the country’s dependence on hydroelectric power. Throughout the first half of 2017,
demonstrations throughout Venezuela intensified, resulting in increased arrests, property crimes, injuries and deaths. In response to continued food shortages, smuggling, crime, violence and gang activity, the National Executive launched a number of
initiatives, including the distribution of food to low-income populations and the development of a national security plan designed to strengthen the implementation of justice and ensure peace in the country.
For more information on food access and distribution, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—Social Programs—Missions*” and for more information on security plan, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—Other
Social Programs and Policies—Political and Social Unrest*.”

In the December 2015 National Assembly elections, members of the Democratic
Unity Roundtable (*Mesa de la Unidad Democrática* or “MUD”) won a majority of the seats in the National Assembly. Following an appeal by National Assembly members of the *Partido Socialista Unido de Venezuela*
(“PSUV”), the Venezuelan Supreme Tribunal of Justice (*Tribunal Supremo de Justicia* or the “Supreme Tribunal”), agreed to review the legality of the elections of four National Assembly members from the State of Amazonas and
issued a temporary injunction preventing them from taking office pending a final ruling. Despite the ruling of the Supreme Tribunal, the four National Assembly members from the State of Amazonas took office in January 2016. Subsequently, in November
2016, three of the Members from the State of Amazonas tendered their resignations, which the National Assembly did not formally accept and prompted a further ruling by the Supreme Tribunal declaring the nullity of all further acts of the National
Assembly due to its improper constitution.

During the course of 2016 and 2017, political and social discontent was exacerbated by the lack of dialogue
between the National Executive and the opposition and conflicts between laws passed by the National Assembly and rulings issued by Supreme Tribunal and President Maduro. Since January 2016, for example, the Supreme Tribunal declared unconstitutional
a number of acts of the National Assembly, including its resolution calling for the removal of President Maduro. For more information on legal challenges to legislation of the National Assembly, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—
Form of Government and Political Parties—Legal Challenges to Legislation*.”

In May 2017, in order to address the Republic’s political,
social and economic challenges, President Maduro called for the election of a National Constituent Assembly (*Asamblea Nacional Constituyente* or “ANC”) for the adoption of a new constitution and nominated members to a Presidential
Commission to determine the process for the elections of ANC members. On July 30, 2017, 545 constituent members of the ANC where elected by 8,089,320 citizens, representing 42% of those eligible to vote, according to data from the National
Electoral Council (*Consejo Nacional Electoral* or “CNE”). In August 2017, the ANC commenced its legislative session and decreed the constitutional supremacy of its position and its legislative acts within the Government and reserved
all necessary powers to legislate on the competences, functioning and organization of all Government branches to preserve the peace, justice and sovereignty of the Republic. For more information on the ANC, see “*Bolivarian Republic of
Venezuela—Form of Government and Political Parties*—*National Constituent Assembly*.”

In 2016 and 2017, President Maduro issued a
series of emergency decrees to address various economic, social and energy crises. These emergency decrees granted a number of additional powers to President Maduro, allowing him, among other things, to dictate economic policies, approve the
Republic’s annual budget and make emergency appropriations accessing funds allocated by budgets from previous years. For more information on emergency decrees, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—Form of Government and Political
Parties—Emergency Decrees*.”

In 2016 and 2017, President Maduro reorganized the executive cabinet and other key government institutions,
appointing or replacing several ministers, including, the Vice President of the Republic, the Vice President for Economic Affairs, the Minister of Finance, the Minister of Petroleum, Minister of Communications and

Information, the Minister of Culture and the President Banco Central. In January 2017, President Maduro merged the Ministry of Popular Power for Banking and Finance and the Ministry of Popular
Power for Industry and Commerce to create the Ministry of Economy and Finance in order to unify the competencies in economics and finance and sectoral policies in the field of industry and commerce. Furthermore, President Maduro created the Ministry
of Transportation and the Ministry of Public Works. For more information on the reorganization of the executive cabinet, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—Form of Government and Political Parties—Current Presidential
Administration*.”

On December 10, 2017, elections were held for the mayors of 335 municipalities and for the governor of the State of Zulia.
According to data published by the CNE, 9,139,564 citizens voted in the elections, representing 47% of those eligible to vote.

***External Affairs***

***United States***

Since July 2014, the
United States has imposed sanctions, consisting of visa restrictions, economic restrictions and asset blocking. These sanctions have been renewed and substantially increased in scope during 2017. In August 2017, the United States imposed additional
sanctions prohibiting, among others, any U.S. participation in the trading or issuance of new debt instruments by the Republic and its controlled entities. For more information on sanctions and the Republic’s relations with the United States,
see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—External Affairs and Membership in International Organizations—Relations with the United States*.”

***Europe and Canada***

In November 2017, the European
Union implemented a sanction regime against the Republic, imposing an arms embargo and a legal framework for the EU and European countries to implement travel bans and asset freezes against certain Venezuelan individuals.

In September 2017, Canada adopted sanctions against 40 Venezuelan officials, imposing an asset freeze and prohibition on Canadian companies and individuals
from certain dealings with those sanctioned.

For more information, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—External Affairs and Membership in
International Organizations—Relations with Europe and Other Western Countries*.”

***Neighboring Countries***

In August 2017, the foreign ministers of Argentina, Brazil, Canada, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Panama, Paraguay and Peru signed
a declaration condemning the constitution of the ANC and rejecting its authority, and advocated for the opening of negotiations between the National Executive and the opposition to address the ongoing constitutional crisis.

The Republic has been part of a border dispute with Guyana that the two countries have agreed to seek to settle diplomatically. As of December 2017, the
conflict has not yet been solved despite the prolonged mediation process. For more information, on Republic’s relations with neighboring countries, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—Geography and Population.*”

***International Organizations***

In December 2016,
Mercosur, a regional trading bloc and customs union, suspended the Republic’s membership in the bloc because it had not fully complied with all the conditions of membership. The Republic disputes the suspension and has filed a request for
dispute resolution under Mercosur’s internal rules.

In February 2017, the United Nations General Assembly announced the suspension of voting rights
of the Republic for the 2016-2017 sessions due to unpaid member dues.

In March 2017, Venezuela joined the Asian Investment and Infrastructure Bank (“AIIB”) to strengthen its
relationship with countries in Asia.

In April 2017, following repeated attempts by certain members of the Organization of American States
(“OAS”) and its secretariat to suspend Venezuela from the OAS due to alleged breaches of the OAS Charter, the National Executive announced the Republic would withdraw from the OAS.

For more information on the Republic’s relations with International Organizations, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—External Affairs
and Membership in International Organizations*.”

***Nationalization and Expropriations***

In February 2017, President Maduro declared that it was in the national interest to expropriate the assets of *Consorcio Industrial Venezolano de
Tecnología China* (“Civetchi”), a vehicle manufacturer. For more information on nationalizations and expropriations, see “*Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela— Other Social Programs and Policies — Nationalizations
and Expropriations of Private Companies*.”

**Recent Economic Developments**

From 2012 to 2016, the economy contracted by
an average annual rate of 3.9%. As of September 30, 2016, GDP for the first nine months of 2016 was Bs.46.9 billion in 1997 Constant Bolívares, a decrease as compared to 2015. The decrease in 2016 was primarily due to decreases in
both the petroleum and non-petroleum sectors.

***The 2017 Budget***

In October 2016, President Maduro signed the 2017 budget, with the approval of the Supreme Tribunal. For further discussion of the approval of the 2017 budget,
see “*The Venezuelan Economy—Economic Policy and Legislation—The Budget and the Pluriannual Framework.*” The 2017 budget, as approved by the Supreme Tribunal, projects total revenues of U.S.$831.1 billion, excluding
borrowing, and total expenditures of U.S.$847.9 billion. The 2017 budget also contemplates a legal limit on borrowing by the Republic of U.S.$16.8 billion. The 2017 budget is based on certain assumptions, including an average price for the
Venezuelan oil basket of U.S.$30.00 per barrel and an average exchange rate of Bs.10.00 = U.S.$1.00.

The 2017 budget estimates that 82.9% of resources is
expected to come from the collection of internal taxes; 11.9% is expected to come from dividends and special contributions of the socialist companies of the state; 2% income from indebtedness; and 3.2% is expected to come from contributions from the
petroleum sector.

***Exchange Control Regime***

During 2016 and 2017, the Ministry of Economy and Finance and Banco Central issued a number of administrative orders (*Convenio Cambiario* or
“Currency Exchange Agreements”) that modified the exchange control regime and establish specific rules for foreign-currency denominated transactions for operators in the tourism and oil and gas sectors as well as those participating in the
export of goods and services.

Beginning in May 2017, Banco Central has administered weekly foreign currency auctions, allowing both individuals and
private businesses to sell and purchase limited amounts of foreign currencies. For more information on the Republic’s currency exchange regime, see “*The Venezuelan Economy—Economic Policy and Legislation—Currency Exchange
Agreements*.”

***Foreign Trade and Balance of Payments***

In October 2016, Venezuela and Turkey entered into a series of bilateral agreements to increase cooperation on matters relating to energy, culture and social
and economic development. These agreements also cover investments by Turkish companies in the José Antonio Anzoátegui Refinery in Venezuela and the construction by PDVSA of a Venezuelan oil storage facility in Turkey.

During 2017, Venezuela and China entered into several cooperation agreements to boost and strengthen the industrial sector in Venezuela, specifically raising
the production of iron ore, bauxite and aluminum.

In February 2017, Venezuela and Jamaica entered into an agreement under PetroCaribe, an international alliance of
Caribbean nations and the Republic for the purchase of oil at preferential conditions, for the upgrade of their jointly owned Kingston, Jamaica refinery. This agreement aims to increase the refinery’s output from 36,000 barrels per day (bdp) to
50,000 bdp. The upgrade is expected to cost between U.S.$850 million and U.S.$1 billion and will be carried out by Chinese state engineering and construction company Sinohydro.

In March 2017, Venezuela and Trinidad and Tobago entered into an agreement for the construction, operation, and maintenance of a new gas pipeline connecting
the two countries. The deal is valued at over U.S.$100 million and will involve the participation of PDVSA, Trinidadian company National Group Gas, and Royal Dutch Shell.

In April 2017, Venezuela and Belarus entered into a cooperation agreements to boost and strengthen the respective countries’ industrial sectors.

In June 2017, as part of efforts by the Ministry of Essential, Strategic and Socialist Industries, Venezuela’s largest steel corporation, SIDOR, entered
into a production agreement with Italian company Danieli to boost production levels.

***Minimum Wage***

During 2016 and 2017, the minimum wage was increased four and six times, respectively, with the latest increase in November 2017, bringing the minimum wage to
Bs.177,507.44 (U.S.$17,750.74). For more information on minimum wages, see “*The Venezuelan Economy—Employment and Labor—Minimum Wages*.”

**Recent Developments in the Sectors of the Venezuela Economy**

**Petroleum and Natural Gas**

***PDVSA reorganization***

Since December 2015, PDVSA and certain of its affiliates, have been subject to corruption investigations, in the United States and in Venezuela. A
number of contractors and suppliers to PDVSA affiliates and a number of PDVSA’s former employees have plead guilty to violating anti-corruption and anti-money laundering laws in the United States. In February 2017, a former PDVSA manager was
charged with corruption in Venezuela. In March 2017, the National Government investigated PDVSA officials for allegedly embezzling public funds and several PDVSA senior executives were arrested and charged with graft over inflated equipment sale
fees. In addition, a senior PDVSA manager was arrested on suspicion of irregularities in contracts to supply fuel to the domestic market. Further investigations were carried out during November 2017 that resulted in the arrest of two former senior
managers of PDVSA that served as presidents of the company between September 2014 and August 2017 and between August 2017 and November 2017, respectively. In December 2017, the Prosecutor General announced it would initiate criminal investigations
against the former Minister of Petroleum and the former president of PDVSA, who served in those positions between November 2004 and September 2014.

As
result, in 2017, President Maduro carried out a reorganization of PDVSA’s corporate governance, creating a vice presidency position and appointing Maribel Parra to that position (Decree No. 2,703, published in the Extraordinary Official
Gazette No. 6,284, dated January 29, 2017). In November 2017, President Maduro appointed Manuel Quevedo as president of PDSVA (Decree No. 3,178, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,343, dated November 26,
2017).

***PDVSA operations***

In October 2016,
PDVSA and Repsol announced an agreement to fund the Petroquiriquire joint venture, of which PDVSA owns 60% and Repsol owns the remaining 40%. Under the agreement, Repsol has committed to provide a credit line of up to U.S.$1.2 billion to the
joint venture over a period of five years.

In November 2016, PDVSA entered into financing deals with ONGC Videsh Limited and DP Delta Finance, B.V.
(“Videsh”), which are intended to boost production in their existing joint ventures to 40,000 bdp and 110,000 bdp, respectively. In January 2017, Videsh received the first installment of U.S.$19 million from PDVSA.

Also in November 2016, PDVSA announced that it had entered into a series of agreements with China National
Petroleum Corporation (“CNPC”), which are intended to increase crude production in Venezuela and trade in petroleum products with China. These agreements contemplate increased investments in existing Venezuelan joint ventures between PDVSA
and CNPC and a new joint venture to develop a refinery in China to process Venezuelan crude oil.

In December 2016, PDVSA and Shell Venezuela entered into
agreements to boost production of their joint venture company, Petroregional del Lago. These agreements contemplate an approximate U.S.$2.8 billion investment in heavy crude production facilities.

In February 2017, PDVSA signed agreements to boost domestic gas production and distribution with Gavenplast, a plastics manufacturing company. These
agreements contemplate the development of several joint ventures projects in the oil sector with a number of Chinese companies.

In March 2017, PDVSA
offered Rosneft, 10% stake in a joint venture with Petropiar, the national oil company of Paraguay.

For more information on PDVSA’s operations, see
“*The Venezuelan Economy—Petroleum and Natural Gas—Trade Agreements*.”

In February 2016, President Maduro announced a program to promote the development of the mining sector, the Mining Engine. The Mining Engine consists of
several key strategic components: (1) the promotion of goods and services necessary to the exploration and exploitation process of minerals such as gold, diamonds and coltan, (2) the development of potential mineral reserves in the Orinoco
Oil Belt, (3) the certification of Venezuela’s mineral reserves, (4) the Law for Productive Mining Development 2016–2018, which reserves to the state strategic mineral exploration and extraction rights, (5) international
agreements to conduct development and exploration activities through joint ventures, and (6) the expansion of international reserves. As part of the Mining Engine, President Maduro created the Orinoco Mining Arc, a National Strategic
Development Zone comprising 111,843.70 square kilometers in the Bolívar State, to exploit the region’s mineral reserves in accordance with sustainable practices and respect for the environment. In addition, the Republic signed agreements
with operators from China, Canada and the Republic of Congo to exploit concessions in the region. For more information on the Venezuelan mining industry, see “*The Venezuelan Economy—Mining*.”

The Republic and its controlled entities are party to a number of disputes with foreign investors in particular in the mining and oil sectors.

***Disputes in the Mining Sector***

In February 2011,
Canadian mining company, Crystallex International Corporation (“Crystallex”), filed a number of claims in various jurisdictions, including the U.S. and Canada and before International Centre for Settlement of Investment Disputes
(“ICSID”) against the Republic, alleging breach of contract and violations of the Bilateral Investment Treaty (“BIT”) following the Republic’s nationalization of a Crystallex gold mine. The dispute was settled in November
2017.

In November 2009, Gold Reserve a mining company, initiated an ICSID arbitration against the Republic for the expropriation of certain Gold Reserve
copper and gold mines. Following protracted litigation, in August 2016, the parties entered into a settlement agreement under which the Republic agreed to pay U.S.$770 million. The settlement agreement was amended in November and December 2016.
Following additional litigation, in June and July 2017, the Republic paid Gold Reserve U.S.$69.5 million pursuant to the terms of the amended settlement agreement.

In August 2012, Canadian mining company, Rusoro Mining Ltd., initiated an ICSID arbitration against the Republic, alleging that the Republic unlawfully
expropriated its investments in violation of the BIT between Canada and Venezuela. In August 2016, the ICSID tribunal issued an award in favor of Rusoro for U.S.$968 million plus interest. In October 2016, the Republic made an application for
annulment of the arbitral award. In February 2017,
the Republic and Rusoro signed an agreement in the amount of U.S.$5.5 million for the issuance of non-interest bearing promissory notes that will be
due after 90 days from the date that Rusoro receives its first payment from the Republic under the arbitral award.

On April 10, 2014, Anglo American
PLC initiated an ICSID arbitration against the Republic for the expropriation of its nickel mining concession in Venezuela. After a written pleadings phase, a hearing of jurisdiction and merits was held in Paris in December 2016. The tribunal’s
decision is pending.

For more information on the Republic’s disputes in the mining sector, see “*The Venezuelan
Economy—Mining—Mining Sector*.”

***Disputes in the Oil Sector***

In September 2007, Mobil Corporation, and certain of its subsidiaries commenced ICSID arbitration proceedings against the Republic. Mobil claims that the
Republic breached provisions under the Netherlands-Venezuela BIT and Venezuela’s Foreign Investment Law when the Republic nationalized and expropriated Mobil’s investments. Following prolonged litigation, in October 2014, the ICSID
tribunal ruled in favor of Mobil, awarding U.S.$1.0 billion. The Republic filed an application for revision of the award that was accepted in part in March 2017, where the arbitral tribunal annulled in part the previous award. In July 2017,
following the appeal by the Republic with respect of the enforcement order filed by Mobil earlier that year, the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit ruled in favor of the Republic, stating that Mobil should have tried to enforce the award
under the federal Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act rather than initiating the process through the U.S. court system.

In October 2016, ConocoPhillips
filed suit against PDVSA and certain of its affiliates in U.S. District Court for the District of Delaware, alleging that PDVSA shifted assets to avoid paying ConocoPhillips for the Republic’s nationalization of oil assets under President
Chávez’s administration. In January 2017, ConocoPhillips filed a second suit against PDVSA and Rosneft in the U.S. District Court for the District of Delaware, in connection with a November 2016 crude oil prepayment transaction pursuant
to which PDVSA granted a lien on 49.9% of its shares in CITGO Holding, Inc. to secure a U.S.$1.5 billion prepayment made by Rosneft. In March 2017, PDVSA filed a motion, claiming immunity from the ConocoPhillips suit under the Foreign Sovereign
Immunities Act, which is currently being disputed by ConocoPhillips, which claims that the Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act does not cover commercial activity.

For more information on the Republic’s disputes in the oil sector, see “*The Venezuelan Economy—Petroleum and Natural Gas—Disputes
Concerning the Oil Industry*.”

***Disputes in other sectors***

On July 25, 2016, Agroinsumos Ibero-Americanos, S.L., a Spanish agriculture company, initiated an ICSID arbitration against the Republic claiming that the
Republic violated a BIT with Spain when it appropriated the company’s investments in two agriculture joint-ventures. The tribunal was constituted on December 22, 2016. The claimants were required to submit their briefs by July 17,
2017 this proceedings is on-going.

In January 2017, Air Canada initiated an ICSID arbitration against the
Republic for the repatriation of proceeds from domestic tickets sales in connection with the losses due to currency controls imposed by the Republic, this proceeding is on-going.

**BOLIVARIAN REPUBLIC OF VENEZUELA**

**Geography and Population**

Venezuela is situated on the northern coast of South America. To the north, it has a coastline of 2,813 kilometers on the Caribbean Sea and the Atlantic Ocean.
Colombia borders it on the west, Brazil on the south and Guyana on the east. Venezuela’s national territory of 916,445 square kilometers includes 72 islands in the Caribbean. The Venezuelan territory varies from tropical to mountainous to
Amazonian regions. Environmentally-protected areas comprise 40% of the Venezuela territory.

Caracas, Venezuela’s capital and largest city, is
Venezuela’s political, financial, commercial, communications and cultural center. According to calculated projections based on the 2011 Census prepared by the INE, Caracas (including the Capital District) had an estimated population of
2,084,074 million in 2016. The population of Maracaibo, the nation’s second-largest city, located near Venezuela’s most important petroleum fields and richest agricultural areas, had an estimated population of 1,678,570 million
in 2016, based on the same data.

Part of the eastern border with Guyana is subject to a border dispute. Venezuela has claimed that certain
territory occupied by Guyana should be considered part of Venezuela’s national territory. The area of dispute is currently under the control of Guyana. Under international accords, Venezuela and Guyana have agreed to seek a settlement of the
territorial dispute. Since the signing of the accords, Venezuela and Guyana have periodically engaged in negotiations regarding the status of the territory. Nevertheless, the negotiations to date have not resulted in a final accord. In May 2015, the
Republic created the Integrated Defense Zone Marine and Insular (“ZODIMAIN”), which claimed the waters off the Essequibo River region in the disputed territory. In July 2015, President Maduro requested that the United Nations mediate the
dispute between Venezuela and Guyana. On July 6, 2015, Venezuela recalled its ambassador to Guyana and in early September 2015, delayed its acceptance of Guyana’s nominee for ambassador to the Republic. On September 27, 2015, the
Republic and Guyana restored their respective ambassadors following a meeting between the President of the Republic, Guyana’s President and U.N. Secretary General Ban Ki-moon in New York. On
December 29, 2016, the United Nations awarded Venezuela and Guyana one final year to settle the border dispute, otherwise the case will be submitted to the International Court of Justice. In March 2017, the secretary of the United Nations
appointed Dag Halvor Nylander to conduct the official process and assist both countries with reaching a resolution regarding the border controversy. In May 2017, Mr. Nylander met with representatives of both countries to discuss the mediation
stages. Although the conflict has not been resolved, Guyana’s government has announced it will drill for oil in the disputed border region.

Drug
traffickers and guerrilla incursions from Colombia and other incidents present a continuing problem along the Venezuela/Colombia border. Venezuela has stationed armed forces on the sparsely-populated western border to control incursions and to
protect Venezuelan ranchers residing in this area. In August 2014, the National Executive announced steps to close the border with Colombia during certain hours at certain times of the day in order to combat smuggling across the border. Subsidized
goods available for sale under the Missions in Venezuela are hoarded and sold at higher prices in Colombia, leading to shortages in Venezuela and contributing to inflationary pressures. On August 19, 2015, President Maduro initially closed two
border crossings to Colombia for 72 hours after three members of the Venezuelan military, who were conducting an anti-smuggling exercise, were shot and killed. Between August and October 2015, the National Executive issued several decrees
declaring a state of emergency in most of the municipalities in the affected states of Táchira, Zulia, Apure and Amazonas in October for an initial period of 60 days, along the Colombian border, closing several border crossings and
restricting the movement of goods and enabling security forces to carry out vehicle checks in efforts to combat smuggling and drug-trafficking. On September 28, 2015, the Republic and the South American Union of Nations (*Unión
Sudamericana de Naciones* or “UNASUR,”) agreed that Colombian citizens who were required to leave Venezuela from August 19, 2015 were permitted to return. The regional state of emergency was extended for subsequent 60-day periods until it was superseded by a nationwide state of emergency instituted on January 14, 2016. Following two temporary border openings in July 2016, in which Venezuelans were permitted to cross into
Colombia temporarily to buy necessities, Venezuela and Colombia agreed to gradually reopen the border starting on August 13, 2016. The reopening of the border is part of a larger framework to create a zone of peace along the border between the
two countries involving five strategic objectives: combatting smuggling, strengthening bilateral trade, customs regulation, transport and health. On December 12, 2016, President Maduro ordered the closure of the border with Colombia for 72
hours to combat currency smuggling in connection with the circulation of the new Bolívares bills. This measure was extended until December 20, 2016, by which time the Colombian and Venezuelan presidents agreed to strengthen border
security and open the binational boundaries. In January 2017, the National Executive opened eight exchange offices on the border with Colombia to promote transparency and legal commerce between the two countries.

Venezuela’s estimated population in 2016 was 31,519,000 million, of which 65.5% were between the ages of 15 and 64 years. According to the latest
available figures from INE, as of December 31, 2015, the poor represented 33.1% of the Venezuelan population. Figures for 2016 are not currently available. The following table sets forth comparative GDP figures for Venezuela and other selected
Latin American countries:

**Form of Government and Political Parties**

Venezuela is
divided into 23 states, the Capital District and various federal dependencies.

Venezuela has had a democratically-elected government since 1958,
following the overthrow of a military dictatorship. The current Constitution, adopted in 1999, establishes the structure of the government, including the division of powers among the executive, legislative, judicial, civic and electoral branches, as
well as individual and collective rights and duties.

On December 16, 2012, the most recent gubernatorial elections were held and candidates from the
PSUV, the party of President Chávez and President Maduro, won 20 of the 23 gubernatorial offices. Candidates associated with opposition parties were elected in States of Miranda, Lara and Amazonas. The subsequent gubernatorial elections were
held in October of 2017, securing 18 gubernatorial offices for the PSUV. On December 10, 2017, elections were held for the mayors of 335 municipalities and for the governor of the State of Zulia. According to data published by the CNE,
9,139,564 citizens voted in the elections, representing 47% of those eligible to vote. The PSUV secured most of the seats, including the gubernatorial office of the State of Zulia.

On March 5, 2013, then Vice President Nicolás Maduro became President of the Republic after the death of former President Hugo Chávez. On
March 8, 2013, the Supreme Tribunal issued a decision confirming that under the Constitution, President Maduro could continue to serve as acting President and be a presidential candidate at the
same time. President Maduro initially served as acting president and was elected to serve the remainder of President Chávez’s six-year term on
April 14, 2013, capturing 50.6% of the vote. In January 2017, President Maduro appointed Tareck Zaidan El Aissami Maddah as Vice President of the Republic (Decree No. 2,652, published in the Official Gazette No. 41,067, dated
January 4, 2017). The next scheduled Presidential elections are expected to be in 2018.

On December 6, 2015, elections were held for all 167
seats in the National Assembly, as discussed below under “—*National Assembly*.”

***Political Parties***

Prior to the mid-1990s, the two largest political parties in Venezuela had been *Acción Democrática*
(“AD”), and *Partido Social Cristiano* (“COPEI”). These parties attracted support from a wide spectrum of political interests. Between 1958 and 1993, representatives of AD held the presidency five times and representatives
of COPEI held the presidency twice. Commencing with the presidential election in 1993, voters became dissatisfied with AD and COPEI and several new coalition parties recorded electoral victories. In 1993, Rafael Caldera was elected President on the
*Convergencia* party ticket. Former President Caldera had previously served as President between 1974 and 1979 as the COPEI candidate. In elections held in December 1998, Hugo Chávez Frias was elected as the candidate from the
*Movimiento Quinta República* party, with support from other minor parties to the left of AD and COPEI. In accordance with the results of a popular referendum held in 1999, a constituent assembly was created to propose a new
constitution. The constituent assembly proposed a new constitution, which was approved by the Venezuelan electorate and became effective on December 30, 1999. President Chávez was subsequently
re-elected as President in elections held in 2000 following the adoption of the new Constitution and again in elections held in 2006 and 2012.

In December 2006, President Chávez proposed the creation of a unified political party, the PSUV, comprised of those political parties that supported
the President. The PSUV officially registered with the National Electoral Council (*Consejo Nacional Electoral*) on April 17, 2008. In 2010, the PSUV joined other political parties that supported President Chávez and created the
*Alianza Patriótica*, or Patriotic Alliance, to campaign for the September 2010 National Assembly elections. Parties that support the President are not obligated to join PSUV and can remain autonomous. The Great Patriotic Pole
Simón Bolívar (*El**Gran Polo Patriótico Simón Bolívar*) is a coalition of political parties and social movements in Venezuela on the left side of the political spectrum that was created in
2012 to support the re-election of President Chávez, which includes the PSUV. The Great Patriotic Pole Simón Bolívar obtained 5,616,639 votes in the 2015 National Assembly elections,
representing 32.93% of the vote, which entitled the coalition to appoint 55 members of the National Assembly.

The MUD was created on January 23,
2008, as an alliance of various parties in opposition to President Chávez. The MUD opposition alliance was restructured on June 8, 2009. MUD held primary elections on February 12, 2012, to select a presidential candidate to run
against President Chávez in the 2012 elections. The candidate elected during the primary elections was Henrique Capriles Radonski, governor of the Miranda State. On October 7, 2012, President Chávez was re-elected President for a six-year term, receiving 55% of the vote. On December 16, 2012, Mr. Capriles was elected Governor of the Miranda State. In the 2015
National Assembly elections, candidates from parties in the MUD opposition alliance obtained 7,482,252 votes, representing 65.27% of the vote, which entitled the MUD opposition alliance to appoint 109 seats.

In 2016, the Supreme Tribunal ordered the CNE to begin a renewal process of political parties through signature collection drives. The signature collection
process requires the collection of signatures from 0.5 percent of registered voters (Rulings No. 1,415 and 878, 2016). The CNE launched this process on February 18, 2017. In April, 2017, the Supreme Tribunal issued a ruling modifying
the renewal procedures established by the CNE to allow parties to count the votes the parties received in the previous election toward the 0.5 percent threshold, in addition to the signatures collected. As of August, 2017, this process has
resulted in 22 eligible parties.

***Current Presidential Administration***

On March 5, 2013, President Chávez died. Pursuant to the Constitution, Vice President Nicolas Maduro became acting interim President and new
elections were planned for April 2013 to elect a president to serve the remainder of
the Presidential term. On March 8, 2013, the Supreme Tribunal issued a decision that Mr. Maduro could serve as acting President and be a presidential candidate at the same time under
the Constitution. Henrique Capriles Radonski temporarily withdrew from his position as governor of Miranda State to run as the opposition candidate to Mr. Maduro. In the election held on April 14, 2013, acting President Maduro was elected
President for a six-year term, obtaining 50.6% of the vote, with Mr. Capriles receiving 49.0% of the votes cast.

The Maduro administration is currently pursuing policies initiated by the Chávez administration. President Maduro presented the Plan of the Nation,
first introduced by President Chávez during his 2012 presidential campaign and later implemented by President Maduro, which is a roadmap for the second stage of Venezuela’s transition to socialism. The Plan of the Nation establishes a
series of macroeconomic targets and social programs and is founded on five strategic objectives: (1) the preservation of Venezuela’s independence; (2) the continued development of Bolivarian socialism for the 21st century;
(3) the transformation of Venezuela into a regional social, economic and political power; (4) contribution to the development of a multipolar geopolitical order to promote peace and stability; and (5) preserving life on earth and
saving the human race.

In September 2014, President Maduro initiated a reform agenda, referred to as the Five Revolutions (*Cinco Revoluciones*)
designed to advance the transformation of the Republic to a communal State and to enhance popular power. The Five Revolutions consist of:

• the Economic Productivity Revolution (Revolución Económica Productiva), which aims to promote economic stability by developing a productive and diverse economy;

• the Knowledge, Science, Culture and Technology Revolution (Revolución del conocimiento, de la ciencia, cultura y tecnología), which aims to develop a new society founded on solidarity, community and development by promoting science, education, culture, technology;

• the Revolution of the Socialist Missions (Revolución de las misiones socialistas), which aims to improve the quality of life for all Venezuelans and create a new society that guarantees basic social rights and life by promoting the Mission system;

• the Revolution of the Political State (Revolución Política del Estado), which aims to transform the Government to create a new administrative state founded in principals of democracy and justice and to increase popular power by implementing measures, including technology, to combat smuggling, corruption and bureaucratic inefficiencies; and

• the Revolution of Territorial Socialism (Revolución del Socialismo Territorial), which aims to consolidate the communal model with environmental principles by establishing a new ecosocialism.

In April 2016, members of MUD began a referendum process to recall President Maduro from office. The Constitution provides that the office
of all magistrates and other elected officials, including the President, can be revoked after the first half of such individual’s term of office has elapsed, provided that certain legal requirements are met. For more information on these
requirements, see “*Organization of the Venezuelan Government—The Executive Branch*.” In May 2016, citizens requested that the CNE certify signatures on a petition to recall President Maduro. Between June 20 and
June 24, 2016, the CNE reviewed and validated the signatures to the petition. On August 1, 2016, the CNE certified signatures on the recall petition representing more than the 1% of registered voters needed to trigger the second stage of
the recall process. On September 21, 2016, the CNE announced that a referendum to recall President Maduro from office could not be held until 2017. On October 17, 2016, the Supreme Tribunal ruled that the MUD opposition alliance must
collect signatures from 20% of voters in each of the Republic’s twenty-three states and the Capital District in order to proceed with a recall referendum. Subsequently, on October 21, 2016, the CNE announced that the recall process was
temporarily suspended after criminal courts in several states agreed to hear claims that fraud had been committed in the gathering of the signatures needed to meet the 1% threshold to trigger the recall process.

In January 2017, President Maduro reorganized the executive cabinet, appointing more than 17 new ministers, to offices including the Vice President of the
Republic, Vice President for Economic Affairs, Minister of Petroleum and Minister of Economy and Finance (Decree No. 2,652, published in the Official Gazette No. 41,067, dated January 4, 2017). Furthermore, since January 2017, the
Ministry of Popular Power for Banking and Finance and the Ministry of Popular Power for Industry and Commerce merged, to create the Ministry of Economy and Finance

(Decree No. 2,651, published in the Official Gazette No. 41,067, dated January 4, 2017) and the National Executive created eight new ministries: (1) the Ministry of
Transportation; (2) the Ministry of Public Works (Decree No. 2,650, published in the Official Gazette No. 41,067, dated January 4, 2017); (3) the Ministry of Productive Economy; (4) the Ministry of Fisheries; (5) the
Ministry of Urban Agriculture; (6) the Ministry of Foreign Trade and International Investment; (7) Ministry of Mining; and (8) the Ministry of Essential, Strategic and Socialist Industries. Currently, there are 32 Ministries, two
State Ministries, one Council of Ministers and five sectoral Vice Presidents consisting of the Vice President for Economic Affairs, the Vice President of Planning, the Vice President for Social Development and Missions of the Revolution, the Vice
President of Political Sovereignty, Security and Peace the Vice President for the Development of Territorial Socialism and the Executive Vice President.

On January 26, 2017, President Maduro granted the following powers to the Executive Vice President of the Republic: (a) decree issuance of
(i) budgetary transfers between the ministries, (ii) the rectification of budgetary insufficiencies of the ministries, (iii) extension of the period of suppression and liquidation of the organs of the National Public Administration,
(iv) appointment of deputy ministers and appointment of the board of directors of the decentralized entities, (v) expropriation and compulsory acquisition requested by the President, (vi) creation, modification, elimination and
liquidation of decentralized entities, except those that must be issued by law, (vii) value added tax (“VAT”) exemptions on the importation of personal property, sale of goods and provision of services, and (viii) exemption from
income tax with respect to the profit obtained by strategic sectors or special projects; (b) approve, defer or reject the administrative acts presented by the ministers, including the administrative act relating to the purchase of foreign
currency of the public sector before Banco Central in relation to Foreign Exchange Agreement No. 11; (c) approve, defer or reject the budget of decentralized entities and state corporations; (d) approve and sign the issuance of special
retirements to employees of the National Public Administration; and (e) perform the role of the President as a member or president in collegiate bodies (Decree No. 2,695, published in the Official Gazette No. 41,083, dated
January 26, 2017).

In January 2017, President Maduro reorganized the board of directors of PDVSA, creating a vice president position and appointing
Maribel Parra as vice president (Decree No. 2,703, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,284, dated January 29, 2017). The new board is comprised of the president, two vice presidents, five internal directors and three
external directors. Eulogio Del Pino was removed as president of the PDVSA in August 2017 and replaced by former Minister of Petroleum, Nelson Martinez. On November 26, 2017, President Maduro removed Nelson Martinez and appointed Manuel Quevedo
as the Minister of Petroleum and president of PDVSA (Decrees Nos. 3,177 and 3,178 published in the Extraordinary Gazette No. 6,343, dated November 26, 2017).

***Organization of the Venezuelan Government***

The
Government is comprised of five branches at the national level, as well as state and local governments. The following is a description of the role of the various branches of Government:

*Federal Government*

**The Executive Branch**.
Executive power is vested in the President and the Vice President, who is appointed by the President. Under the Constitution, the President is elected for a term of six years and may be re-elected for
unlimited six-year terms. The Council of Ministers is also part of the executive branch. Under the Constitution, the President is the
commander-in-chief of Venezuela’s armed forces. The different services within Venezuela’s armed forces report to the Minister of Defense.

The Constitution provides that the public can call for a legally-binding recall referendum at any time after the midpoint of the President’s six-year term. To initiate the recall process, 1% of registered voters must sign a petition that is submitted to the CNE. The CNE must certify these signatures and then, within three days of such certification, 20%
of all registered voters must sign a second petition. After those signatures are certified, a referendum may be held. To succeed in recalling an official, the votes in favor of recall must equal or exceed the number of votes the official received in
the initial election and a number of voters equal to or greater than 25% of the total number of registered voters must vote in the recall referendum. If a recall removes a sitting president from office, new elections will be held if the recall
occurred during the first four years of the President’s current term. If the recall removes the President during the final two years of the term, the Vice President will serve out the term until the next election.

On January 9, 2017, the National Assembly passed a resolution stating that President Maduro should be
removed from office based on article 233 of the Bolivarian Constitution, which outlines the circumstances under which the President can be considered to have permanently vacated or abandoned his position, finding that President Maduro had failed to
fulfill his duties. On January 29, 2017, the Supreme Tribunal declared that there were no constitutional grounds for the removal of President Maduro and invalidated the National Assembly’s resolution.

**The Legislative Branch**. National legislative power is vested in the National Assembly. National Assembly members are elected by universal suffrage for
terms of five years and may be re-elected for unlimited five-year terms. The number of members of the National Assembly is determined by the National Electoral Council on the basis of proportional
representation by state.

**The Judicial Branch**. Judicial power is vested in the Supreme Tribunal and various lower tribunals. The Supreme Tribunal
is the final court of appeals. It has the power to declare null and void laws, regulations and other acts of the executive or legislative branches that conflict with the Constitution. The Constitution provides that the National Assembly will appoint
the justices of the Supreme Tribunal for twelve-year terms. Initially, the Supreme Tribunal Law (*Ley orgánica del* *Tribunal Supremo de Justicia*) provided for 20 judges of the Supreme Tribunal. In May 2004, the National Assembly
approved an amendment to the Supreme Tribunal Law which increased the number of judges to 32. An absolute majority of the National Assembly can fill the new positions created by the amendment.

On December 20, 2015, the National Assembly appointed 34 new judges to the Supreme Tribunal, consisting of 13 principal judges and 21 substitutes.

On January 7, 2016, the newly elected National Assembly created a special commission to review these judicial appointments made by the prior National
Assembly to ensure they complied with the proper procedures. On July 7, 2016, the commission issued a final report calling for the annulment of the appointments of the 34 judges. On July 19, 2016, the Supreme Tribunal nullified the actions
of the National Assembly in creating the commission and the actions of the commission, finding that the National Assembly lacked the authority to review and revoke the judicial appointments.

**The Civic Branch**. The civic branch is responsible for preventing, monitoring and sanctioning ethical and moral violations in connection with public
administration. This branch is comprised of three entities: the Ombudsman (*Defensoría del Pueblo*), which promotes and monitors the protection of human rights; the Prosecutor General’s Office, which promotes the fair administration
of justice and judicial processes; and the Comptroller General of the Republic (*Contraloría General de la República*), which monitors and controls the administration of the Republic’s assets, revenues and public debt. The
heads of these entities are appointed by the National Assembly. Candidates are evaluated and qualified by a committee of the National Assembly based on various criteria, such as education level and experience. Appointments are for seven-year terms.
In December 2014, the National Assembly selected new appointees for the positions of Ombudsman, Prosecutor General’s and Comptroller General of the Republic for the period of 2015–2022.

**The Electoral Branch**. The electoral branch, which was created under the Constitution, is responsible for promulgating rules and regulations concerning
elections. The electoral branch also monitors electoral processes, campaign financing and campaign advertising. The electoral branch operates through the CNE. The head and board of directors of the CNE are appointed by a two-thirds majority vote of the National Assembly. In the absence of a two-thirds majority, the Supreme Tribunal may appoint members of the CNE. The most recent appointments
to the CNE in December 2016, were made by the Supreme Tribunal.

*State Government*

State executive power is exercised by a Governor who is elected by universal suffrage within each state. State legislative power is vested in state assemblies
whose members are also elected by universal suffrage within each state.

*Local Government*

**Mayors and Local Councils**.Local executive power is exercised by local mayors who are elected by universal suffrage within each municipality.
Local legislative power is vested in local assemblies whose members are also elected by universal suffrage within each state.

**Popular Power**. On
December 15, 2015, the National Assembly created the first National Community Parliament (*Parlamento Comunal Nacional*), which brought together representatives of Venezuela’s grassroots decision-making bodies, known as communal
councils and communes, to promote the expression of popular power within communities.

***National Assembly***

On December 6, 2015, elections were held for all 167 seats in the National Assembly. The PSUV won 55 seats, members of the MUD opposition alliance won 109
seats and indigenous parties aligned with MUD won the remaining 3 seats. In December 2015, members of the PSUV challenged the election of eight National Assembly members (five from the State of Aragua, two from the State of Amazonas and one from the
State of Yaracuy). In December 2015, the challenges to the members from the State of Aragua and Yaracuy were dismissed by the Supreme Tribunal. On January 11, 2016, the Supreme Tribunal agreed to hear the remaining two challenges and expanded
the scope of the inquiry to the election results from the State of Amazonas including one indigenous seat located in the State. Three of the Assembly members whose seats are under review are members of parties aligned with MUD and one is a member of
a party aligned with the PSUV. The Supreme Tribunal issued a temporary injunction prohibiting these four legislators from the State of Amazonas (including two legislators whose seats were not initially challenged) from taking office until the
Supreme Tribunal had ruled on the matter. On July 28, 2016, the National Assembly reinstated three of these legislators in violation of the injunction, despite the fact that the Supreme Tribunal ruled this was an unconstitutional exercise of
the National Assembly’s authority. Subsequently, on November 15, 2016, the three legislators from Amazonas tendered their resignations as members of the National Assembly but the National Assembly did not formally accept the resignations.
On January 9, 2017, the National Assembly removed the three legislators from Amazonas; however the Supreme Tribunal nullified this parliamentary act as it was passed by an illegitimate Board of Directors of the National Assembly. As of
September 2017, although the three legislators from Amazonas stepped down, the four remaining challenges are still pending before the Supreme Tribunal. The next elections for the National Assembly are due to be held in 2021.

While a three-fifths majority is required to enact enabling laws (*Leyes Habilitantes*), legislation that gives the President the power to issue laws
without the approval of the National Assembly, a two-thirds majority is required to pass organic laws (*Leyes Orgánicas*), laws related to the structure and function of the Republic. A simple
majority is required to approve ordinary laws. An absolute majority of the members present is required to override a presidential veto. Currently, the MUD opposition alliance has enough seats in the National Assembly to pass ordinary laws. Most of
the National Assembly Committees are led by the MUD opposition alliance. The next elections for the National Assembly are expected to be held in 2021. The following table sets forth the number and party affiliations of the National Assembly as of
December 31, 2016:

| Political Party(1) | No. of Seats |
| --- | --- |
| PSUV | 52 |
| Partido Comunista de Venezuela(2) | 2 |
| Vanguardia Bicentenaria Republicana(2) | 1 |
| Primero Justicia(3) | 33 |
| Acción Democratica(3) | 26 |
| Un Nuevo Tiempo(3) | 22 |
| Voluntad Popular(3) | 15 |
| Representación Indígena(3) | 3 |
| Others(3) | 13 |
| Total | 167 |

(1) Four National Assembly seats from the State of Amazonas are under review by the Supreme Tribunal.

(2) Aligned with the PSUV.

(3) Aligned with the MUD opposition alliance.

**National Constituent Assembly**

In May 2017, in order to address the Republic’s political, social and economic challenges, President Maduro called for the election of the ANC and
nominated members to a Presidential Commission to determine the process for the elections of ANC members (Decree No. 2,830, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,295, dated May 1, 2017). In accordance with article 347
of the Constitution, the ANC is entrusted with the power to reorganize the Government, create a new democratic legal order and draft a new constitution. President Maduro outlined the programmatic objectives of the initiative, mandating the ANC to
introduce measures to, among other things, protect the Republic’s sovereignty; re-establish justice and peace in the Republic; transition the Venezuelan economic system into a diversified, productive and
sustainable for a post-oil economy; and improve the Venezuelan judicial and criminal system to better address violence, drug trafficking and corruption and protect human rights and the environment. On
May 23, 2017, President Maduro adopted the election rules, establishing that the 545 members of the ANC would be constituted by 364 seats elected from geographic constituencies representing the Republic’s 23 states and the Capital
District; eight seats would elected among the indigenous communities; and the remaining 173 seats from functional constituencies representing various stakeholders from the Venezuelan civil and business communities (Decree No. 2,878, published
in the Official Gazette No. 41,156, dated May 23, 2017). On July 30, 2017, 537 of the 545 constituent members of the ANC where elected by 8,089,320 citizens, representing 42% of those eligible to vote, according to data from the CNE.
Members for the remaining eight indigenous seats were elected on August 1, 2017 in accordance with the local customs of the indigenous people represented.

In August 2017, the ANC commenced its legislative session and decreed the constitutional supremacy of its position and its legislative acts within the
Government and reserved all necessary powers to legislate on the competences, functioning and organization of all branches of government to preserve the Republic’s peace, justice and sovereignty (Constitutional Decree published in the
Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,323, dated May 23, 2017). Since August 2017, the ANC passed a number of constitutional laws, including the Constitutional Law establishing the commission for trust, justice, peace and public tranquility
(Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,323, dated May 23, 2017); the Constitutional Law against hatred, for the peaceful co-habitation and tolerance (Official Gazette No. 41,274, dated
November 8, 2017); and the Constitutional Law establishing the principles determining fixed prices of certain goods and services (Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,342, dated November 22, 2017). In August 2017, the ANC also
appointed Tarek William Saab as Prosecutor General, removing the sitting Prosecutor General, Linda Marvelia Ortega Diaz (Constitutional Decree, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,322, dated August 5, 2017).

**Enabling Laws**

In December 2010, the National Assembly
passed the 2010 Enabling Law, granting President Chávez the power to legislate and approve laws by decree for a period of 18 months in a number of areas, including, among others, infrastructure, transportation, housing and public services.
The passage of the 2010 Enabling Law resulted in part from the need for the National Executive to act quickly and decisively in response to record rains and floods that left thousands of Venezuelans without homes, jobs and access to basic services
and seriously affected agricultural production. For the period during which the 2010 Enabling Law was in effect until its expiration in June 2012, President Chávez issued 54 decrees for the following purposes, among others:

• addressing the need for permanent housing for those persons who lost their homes to floods or mudslides;

• creating an emergency fund to direct resources towards large-scale housing projects. For information about the emergency fund, refer to “Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—Housing Programs”;

• a law on fair costs and prices that, as of November 2011, created a new commission to regulate prices and profit margins;

• creating the Socialist Agricultural Development Fund (Fondo para el Desarrollo Agrario Socialista or “FONDAS”) for the reconstruction of the agricultural sector;

• strengthening the Institutional Law of the Banking Sector (Ley de Reforma Parcial de la Ley de Instituciones del Sector Bancario);

• to increase protection against fraud;

• providing persons of limited resources in Venezuela with decent housing through an initiative called the “Great Housing Mission Venezuela” (La Gran Misión Vivienda Venezuela);

• amending the existing labor law to improve labor conditions for workers;

• nationalizing several companies in key sectors of the economy to improve productivity;

• creating the State Council to address matters of national interest; and

• reforming the Organic Law of the Financial Administration of the Public Sector (Ley Orgánica de la Administración Financiera del Sector Público or “the Public Sector Financial Administration Law”) to allow the National Executive to issue debt that exceeds the limits established by the annual Special Debt Laws in order to cover extraordinary expenses in emergency situations. For more information on the Public Sector Financial Administration Law, see “The Venezuelan Economy—Economic Policy and Legislation— Financial Administration of the Public Sector.”

In November 2013, the National Assembly passed the 2013 Enabling Law, granting President Maduro the power to legislate by decree on economic and other matters
for a period of twelve months, without requiring the separate approval of the National Assembly. The 2013 Enabling Law was enacted to permit the National Executive to fight corruption and take quick, decisive action on economic matters. Through
November 2014, President Maduro issued decrees covering a number of domestic security and economic areas, including, the following:

• The law establishing the National Center for Foreign Trade (Centro Nacional de Comercio Exterior or “CENCOEX”) and Venezuelan Foreign Trade Corporation (Corporación Venezolana de Comercio Exterior or “CORPOVEX”), published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,116, Decree No. 601, dated November 29, 2013, aimed to manage all import and export activities of Venezuela. CENCOEX was created for the purpose of developing and implementing policies on foreign exchange, exports, imports and foreign investment into the country and investments abroad. CORPOVEX was created to oversee and rationalize imports, ensure that the Republic obtained the best quality and price of imported products and goods; achieve maximum efficiency in the country’s import/export process and centralize the provision of non-oil exports. For more information, see “Introduction and Recent Developments—Recent Economic Developments” and “The Venezuelan Economy—Economic Policy and Legislation—Exchange Control Regime.”

• The Organic Law of Fair Prices superseded the Law on Fair Costs and Prices, published in the Official Gazette No. 40,340, Decree No. 600, dated January 23, 2014, and aimed to reduce inflationary pressures by supplementing existing regulations on fair pricing. The law established a maximum profit of 30% for each entity in the supply chain. The law also created the Superintendency for the Defense of Socio-Economic Rights (Superintendencia Nacional para la Defensa de los Derechos Socioeconómicos or “SUNDDE”) by merging the Institute for Defense of People in the Access to Goods and Services (Instituto para la Defensa de las Personas en el Acceso a los Bienes y Servicios or “INDEPABIS”) and the Superintendency for Fair Costs and Prices (Superintendencia Nacional de Costos y Precios “SUNDECOP”), the governmental entities previously charged with verification of fair prices. SUNDDE’s objectives include: (1) determining fair prices for goods and services through cost structure analysis; (2) establishing a profit ceiling; and (3) controlling economic and commercial activity in order to protect citizens’ incomes and wages. Decree No. 1,467 amended the Organic Law of Fair Prices (published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,156, dated November 19, 2014) to (a) increase the penalties for violations of the fair price rules; (b) create the National Intendancy for the Protection of Worker’s Salary (Intendencia Nacional para la protección del salario del Obreno y Obrera) to provide a legal platform for workers to file claims against businesses that engage in alleged hoarding or speculation and (c) provide a mechanism for the continual adjustment of fair pricing in all stages of the chain of production, distribution, importation, transportation and marketing of goods and services. The Organic Law of Fair Prices was further amended by Decree No. 2,092 Organic Law of Fair Prices (Ley Orgánica de los Precios Justos) under which (1) SUNDDE has the authority to establish or modify the prices of goods and services; (2) SUNDDE and CENCOEX are

jointly tasked with ensuring that the cost of a good or service conforms to the established rate; (3) penalties for violations of the fair price laws were increased; and (4) additional sanctions were enacted (published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,202, dated November 8, 2015). For more information, see “The Venezuelan Economy—Inflation.”

• The Law Regulating Real Estate Leases for Commercial Use (published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 40,418, Decree No. 929, dated May 23, 2014), imposed limits on the amount of rent commercial property owners may charge. The law allows the lessor and lessee to choose among a fixed rental payment, variable rental payment based on the percentage of sales, or a mixed rental payment (including fixed and variable rental payments components). The law also provides formulas and guidelines to determine the rental amounts assessable.

• The Law of the Exchange Regime and its Crimes (published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,150, Decree No. 1,403, dated November 18, 2014), provided that (i) any person who uses foreign currency acquired for a purpose prohibited by the Republic’s exchange rate mechanism may be punished by imprisonment for between two to six years and a fine of 0.5 taxable units per U.S. dollar or equivalent foreign currency; (ii) the falsification or forgery of financial statements may be punished by imprisonment for between one to three years and a fine of 0.5 taxable units per U.S. dollar or equivalent foreign currency; (iii) those who directly or indirectly promote or encourage the commission of illegal acts may be punished by imprisonment for between two to six years and a fine of 0.5 taxable units per dollar or equivalent currency. Decree No. 2,167 amended the Law of the Exchange Regime and its Crimes, as published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,210, dated December 30, 2015. The new law (1) repealed the Law of the Exchange Regime and its Crimes of 2014; (2) mandated the creation of a production certificate system requiring importers to obtain production certificates to import certain supplies; and (3) criminalized the use of exchange rates other than the official exchange rates. For more information, see “The Venezuelan Economy—Economic Policy and Legislation—Exchange Control Regime—Law of the Exchange Regime and its Crimes.”

• The Organic Law of Missions, Great Missions and Micro-Missions (published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,154, dated November 19, 2014), established social programs aimed in the areas of education, food, health and others, to improve the social welfare of poor Venezuelans. Micro-Missions focus on resolving discrete problems that confront communities from time to time. Missions focus on addressing the root causes of poverty in order to increase the social welfare of Venezuelan citizens. Great Missions focus on resolving socioeconomic problems that require collaboration between different sectors of the economy and Government agencies. For more information, see “Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela—Social Programs—Missions.”

• Regulations under the Law of Disarmament, Arms Control and Ammunition, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,129, Decree No. 881, dated April 8, 2014, aims to regulate and control the use, registration, manufacture, sale, supply, storage, registration, import, export, transit and transportation of all kinds of weapons, ammunition and components.

In March 2015, the National Assembly passed the Enabling Anti-Imperialist Act (*Ley Habilitante Antiimperialista para la Paz*) published in the
Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,178 dated March 15, 2015, granting President Maduro the authority to pass laws by presidential decree between March 15 and December 31, 2015, on matters relating to the economy and other
areas. Between December 28 and December 30, 2015, President Maduro exercised this authority to issue a number of decrees, including the following:

• Reforms to the Law of the Central Bank of Venezuela (Ley del Banco Central de Venezuela) (Decree No. 2,179, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,211, dated December 30, 2015), which (1) gives the President the power to appoint the president of Banco Central; (2) eliminates the need for the approval of the National Assembly in connection with appointments and removals of directors of Banco Central and (3) gives Banco Central the authority to regulate the import or export of or other trade in Venezuelan and foreign currency.

• Reforms to the Income Tax Law (Reforma Parcial de la Ley de Impuesto sobre la Renta) (Decree No. 2,163, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,210, dated December 30, 2015), which (1) imposes new measures to limit tax deductions; (2) modifies inflation adjustments to promote the banking, financial and insurance sectors; (3) streamlines the tax regime applicable to cooperative associations and foundations and rebates for investment; and (4) regulates the availability of revenues.

• The Law of Taxation on Major Financial Transactions (Ley de Impuesto a las Grandes Transacciones Financieras) (Decree No. 2,169, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,210, dated December 30, 2015), which imposes a tax on certain financial transactions, such as withdrawals from call deposits and money market funds, certain transfers of checks, securities and negotiable financial instruments and certain inter-bank loan operations.

• Reforms to the Law of Exchange Regime and its Crimes (Ley del Régimen Cambiario y sus Ilicitos) (Decree No. 2,167, published in the Extraordinary Official Gazette No. 6,210, dated December 30, 2015), which (1) repeal the Law of the Exchange Regime and its Crimes of 2014; (2) mandate the creation of a production certificate system requiring importers to obtain production certificates to import certain supplies; (3) criminalize the use of exchange rates other than the official exchange rates; (4) simplify the process for obtaining foreign currency and (5) establish an alternative currency market.
